It’s not only work, even though work is probably the easiest example to point to.\n\nMy mind kind of jumps around—from contract stuff to the mortgage, to whether people in the Bethesda tech circles are looking at me like I’m… I don’t know, damaged goods or something. And then something small, like a recruiter taking longer than usual to email me back, can start feeling like evidence that my whole career is closing down.\n\nI know that probably sounds a little extreme when I say it out loud, but that’s where my head goes.\n\nDoctor: When the worrying starts, do you feel able to stop or control it?\n\nPatient: Not really… I mean, I try to. I’ll tell myself I’m being reasonable, that it’s probably just a delayed email or something normal, but once it starts, it’s like my brain keeps building the next worst-case step.\n\nIt can start with one thing, like a client not getting back to me, and pretty quickly I’m thinking I’ll lose the contract, then I’ll fall behind on divorce payments, and then I’m never going to get back into a real leadership role. I know that sounds like a lot, but in the moment I can’t seem to shut it off.\n\nDoctor: Did these worries begin after a specific stressful event in your life?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think the clearest starting point was when I got fired, about thirteen months ago.\n\nI don’t want to make it sound like I completely fell apart or anything, but that’s when this kind of worrying really started, and it just… never fully eased up. I know that sounds like a lot, but in the moment I can’t seem to shut it off.\n\nDoctor: Did these worries begin after a specific stressful event in your life?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think the clearest starting point was when I got fired, about thirteen months ago.\n\nI don’t want to make it sound like I completely fell apart or anything, but that’s when this kind of worrying really started, and it just… never fully eased up. Even after I picked up contract work, I kept waiting for my head to settle down, and it didn’t really happen.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever get unwanted thoughts that feel intrusive or repetitive, even when they don’t make sense to you?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure… not in the sense of thoughts that feel bizarre or totally out of nowhere, I don’t think.\n\nI do get stuck on the same worries, obviously, but they usually feel tied to real-life stuff, even if I’m probably making too much of them. So… I don’t know if that’s what you mean by intrusive. I wouldn’t want to overstate it.\n\nDoctor: Over the past couple of weeks, have you been feeling down or depressed most of the day?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so… not most of the day, at least. But no, I haven’t really noticed being down all day for days at a time.\n\nDoctor: Have these worries been happening more days than not for at least six months?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think so. Yeah… I mean, I’d like to say I’m still keeping things together, and I am, mostly. But if I’m being honest, it is getting in the way.

Even after I picked up contract work, I kept waiting for my head to settle down, and it didn’t really happen.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever get unwanted thoughts that feel intrusive or repetitive, even when they don’t make sense to you?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure… not in the sense of thoughts that feel bizarre or totally out of nowhere, I don’t think.\n\nI do get stuck on the same worries, obviously, but they usually feel tied to real-life stuff, even if I’m probably making too much of them. So… I don’t know if that’s what you mean by intrusive. I wouldn’t want to overstate it.\n\nDoctor: Over the past couple of weeks, have you been feeling down or depressed most of the day?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so… not most of the day, at least. I mean, I have moments where I feel pretty discouraged or worn down, but I wouldn’t want to call it depressed if that’s not accurate.\n\nMaybe more stressed than sad, if that makes sense? But no, I haven’t really noticed being down all day for days at a time.\n\nDoctor: Have these worries been happening more days than not for at least six months?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think so. I mean, I don’t want to make it sound overly dramatic, but it’s been going on since I got fired about thirteen months ago.\n\nEven after I found contract work, it didn’t really let up the way I thought it would. So yes, more days than not, I’d say.\n\nDoctor: Right now, does most of your worrying still center on losing that job and its consequences?\n\nPatient: Yes… mostly, I think. I mean, it’s not like I sit there only replaying the firing itself all day, but it kind of branches out from there.\n\nMy mind jumps from whether the contract work will hold up, to the mortgage, to… honestly, whether people in the Bethesda tech circles see me as damaged goods now. And then something small, like a recruiter taking too long to email back, can suddenly feel like proof that my whole career is closing down.\n\nI know that probably sounds excessive. I’m trying not to make it bigger than it is, but yes, it’s still centered around that and what it could mean.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel restless or on edge?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I guess I do. I mean, I try not to make a big deal out of it, but I feel kind of wound up almost all day.\n\nIt’s like my shoulders are locked, and I’m just… braced for bad news, even when nothing is actually happening. I’ll catch myself pacing around the house or bouncing my leg during calls. I don’t always notice it until someone points it out or I realize I’ve been doing it for a while.\n\nSo yes, on edge is probably fair.\n\nDoctor: Would you say your level of anxiety has been much stronger than most people would have after losing a job?\n\nPatient: I… yeah, I think it probably has been stronger than what would be typical, though I don’t love saying that.\n\nIt’s not just “I lost a job, that was stressful.” My mind keeps jumping from the contract work to mortgage payments to whether people in the Bethesda tech circles think I’m damaged goods. And then something small, like a recruiter taking too long to email back, can suddenly feel like evidence that my whole career is closing down.\n\nI know that may sound a bit much, but… yes, I’d say it’s more intense than it probably should be.\n\nDoctor: Do you often notice your muscles feeling tense or tight?\n\nPatient: Yes… I do. I mean, I’m not sure how much is “normal,” but my shoulders especially feel tight a lot, like I’m sort of bracing myself.\n\nI’ll realize I’ve been sitting there with my jaw or shoulders clenched, or I’m pacing around the house, bouncing my leg on calls. It’s not like anything is happening right then, but my body feels like it’s waiting for bad news. Does that make sense?\n\nDoctor: Is this anxiety interfering with your day-to-day functioning?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I mean, I’d like to say I’m still keeping things together, and I am, mostly. But if I’m being honest, it is getting in the way.\n\nI’ve missed some networking follow-ups because I get stuck thinking through everything that could go wrong before I even send a message. And I’ve delayed a couple of client deliverables—not because I don’t care, but because I sort of freeze, overchecking or anticipating problems.\n\nI’ve also pulled back from soccer and seeing friends. I feel embarrassed by how tense and irritable I’ve been, and I don’t want to be… that guy, if that makes sense. So yes, it’s interfering more than I’d like to admit.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"
  },

So yes, more days than not, I’d say.\n\nDoctor: Right now, does most of your worrying still center on losing that job and its consequences?\n\nPatient: Yes… mostly, I think. I mean, it’s not like I sit there only replaying the firing itself all day, but it kind of branches out from there.\n\nMy mind jumps from whether the contract work will hold up, to the mortgage, to… honestly, whether people in the Bethesda tech circles see me as damaged goods now. And then something small, like a recruiter taking too long to email back, can suddenly feel like proof that my whole career is closing down.\n\nI know that probably sounds excessive. I’m trying not to make it bigger than it is, but yes, it’s still centered around that and what it could mean.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel restless or on edge?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I guess I do. I mean, I try not to make a big deal out of it, but I feel kind of wound up almost all day.\n\nIt’s like my shoulders are locked, and I’m just… braced for bad news, even when nothing is actually happening. I’ll catch myself pacing around the house or bouncing my leg during calls. I don’t always notice it until someone points it out or I realize I’ve been doing it for a while.\n\nSo yes, on edge is probably fair.\n\nDoctor: Would you say your level of anxiety has been much stronger than most people would have after losing a job?\n\nPatient: I… yeah, I think it probably has been stronger than what would be typical, though I don’t love saying that.\n\nIt’s not just “I lost a job, that was stressful.” My mind keeps jumping from the contract work to mortgage payments to whether people in the Bethesda tech circles think I’m damaged goods. And then something small, like a recruiter taking too long to email back, can suddenly feel like evidence that my whole career is closing down.\n\nI know that may sound a bit much, but… yes, I’d say it’s more intense than it probably should be.\n\nDoctor: Do you often notice your muscles feeling tense or tight?\n\nPatient: Yes… I do. I mean, I’m not sure how much is “normal,” but my shoulders especially feel tight a lot, like I’m sort of bracing myself.\n\nI’ll realize I’ve been sitting there with my jaw or shoulders clenched, or I’m pacing around the house, bouncing my leg on calls. It’s not like anything is happening right then, but my body feels like it’s waiting for bad news. Does that make sense?\n\nDoctor: Is this anxiety interfering with your day-to-day functioning?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I mean, I’d like to say I’m still keeping things together, and I am, mostly. So yes, it’s interfering more than I’d like to admit.\n\nDoctor: Aside from job loss and its consequences, do you find yourself worrying excessively about other areas of life too?\n\nPatient: Mostly it circles around the job situation, but… it doesn’t stay neatly in that box, if that makes sense.\n\nMy mind jumps from the contract work to the mortgage, and then to what people in the Bethesda tech circles might be thinking about me—like whether I look like damaged goods now. And then something small, like a recruiter taking too long to email back, can suddenly feel like proof that my whole career is closing down.\n\nI know that probably sounds excessive. I’m trying not to make it bigger than it is, but yes, it’s still centered around that and what it could mean.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel restless or on edge?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I guess I do. I mean, I try not to make a big deal out of it, but I feel kind of wound up almost all day.\n\nIt’s like my shoulders are locked, and I’m just… braced for bad news, even when nothing is actually happening. I’ll catch myself pacing around the house or bouncing my leg during calls. I don’t always notice it until someone points it out or I realize I’ve been doing it for a while.\n\nSo yes, on edge is probably fair.\n\nDoctor: Would you say your level of anxiety has been much stronger than most people would have after losing a job?\n\nPatient: I… yeah, I think it probably has been stronger than what would be typical, though I don’t love saying that.\n\nIt’s not just “I lost a job, that was stressful.” My mind keeps jumping from the contract work to mortgage payments to whether people in the Bethesda tech circles think I’m damaged goods. And then something small, like a recruiter taking too long to email back, can suddenly feel like evidence that my whole career is closing down.\n\nI know that may sound a bit much, but… yes, I’d say it’s more intense than it probably should be.\n\nDoctor: Do you often notice your muscles feeling tense or tight?\n\nPatient: Yes… I do. I mean, I’m not sure how much is “normal,” but my shoulders especially feel tight a lot, like I’m sort of bracing myself.\n\nI’ll realize I’ve been sitting there with my jaw or shoulders clenched, or I’m pacing around the house, bouncing my leg on calls. It’s not like anything is happening right then, but my body feels like it’s waiting for bad news. Does that make sense?\n\nDoctor: Is this anxiety interfering with your day-to-day functioning?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I mean, I’d like to say I’m still keeping things together, and I am, mostly. But if I’m being honest, it is getting in the way.\n\nI’ve missed some networking follow-ups because I get stuck thinking through everything that could go wrong before I even send a message. And I’ve delayed a couple of client deliverables—not because I don’t care, but because I sort of freeze, overchecking or anticipating problems.\n\nI’ve also pulled back from soccer and seeing friends. I feel embarrassed by how tense and irritable I’ve been, and I don’t want to be… that guy, if that makes sense. So yes, it’s interfering more than I’d like to admit.\n\nDoctor: Aside from job loss and its consequences, do you find yourself worrying excessively about other areas of life too?\n\nPatient: Mostly it circles around the job situation, but… it doesn’t stay neatly in that box, if that makes sense.\n\nMy mind jumps from the contract work to the mortgage, and then to what people in the Bethesda tech circles might be thinking about me—like whether I look like damaged goods now. I’d like to think it would, yeah. I mean, if work felt secure again, I’m sure a big part of this would calm down.

But… honestly, I’m not sure it would all just disappear. My mind seems to latch onto things and then connect them—contract work, the mortgage, what people in the Bethesda tech circles think of me. So… I don’t know if that’s what you mean by intrusive. I wouldn’t want to overstate it.\n\nDoctor: Over the past couple of weeks, have you been feeling down or depressed most of the day?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so… not most of the day, at least. I mean, I have moments where I feel pretty discouraged or worn down, but I wouldn’t want to call it depressed if that’s not accurate.\n\nMaybe more stressed than sad, if that makes sense? But no, I haven’t really noticed being down all day for days at a time.\n\nDoctor: Have these worries been happening more days than not for at least six months?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think so. I mean, I don’t want to make it sound overly dramatic, but it’s been going on since I got fired about thirteen months ago.\n\nEven after I found contract work, it didn’t really let up the way I thought it would. So yes, more days than not, I’d say.\n\nDoctor: Right now, does most of your worrying still center on losing that job and its consequences?\n\nPatient: Yes… mostly, I think. I mean, it’s not like I sit there only replaying the firing itself all day, but it kind of branches out from there.\n\nMy mind jumps from whether the contract work will hold up, to the mortgage, to… honestly, whether people in the Bethesda tech circles see me as damaged goods now. And then something small, like a recruiter taking too long to email back, can suddenly feel like proof that my whole career is closing down.\n\nI know that probably sounds excessive. I’m trying not to make it bigger than it is, but yes, it’s still centered around that and what it could mean.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel restless or on edge?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I guess I do. I mean, I try not to make a big deal out of it, but I feel kind of wound up almost all day.\n\nIt’s like my shoulders are locked, and I’m just… braced for bad news, even when nothing is actually happening. I’ll catch myself pacing around the house or bouncing my leg during calls. I don’t always notice it until someone points it out or I realize I’ve been doing it for a while.\n\nSo yes, on edge is probably fair.\n\nDoctor: Would you say your level of anxiety has been much stronger than most people would have after losing a job?\n\nPatient: I… yeah, I think it probably has been stronger than what would be typical, though I don’t love saying that.\n\nIt’s not just “I lost a job, that was stressful.” My mind keeps jumping from the contract work to mortgage payments to whether people in the Bethesda tech circles think I’m damaged goods. And then something small, like a recruiter taking too long to email back, can suddenly feel like evidence that my whole career is closing down.\n\nI know that may sound a bit much, but… yes, I’d say it’s more intense than it probably should be.\n\nDoctor: Do you often notice your muscles feeling tense or tight?\n\nPatient: Yes… I do. I mean, I’m not sure how much is “normal,” but my shoulders especially feel tight a lot, like I’m sort of bracing myself.\n\nI’ll realize I’ve been sitting there with my jaw or shoulders clenched, or I’m pacing around the house, bouncing my leg on calls. It’s not like anything is happening right then, but my body feels like it’s waiting for bad news. Does that make sense?\n\nDoctor: Is this anxiety interfering with your day-to-day functioning?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I mean, I’d like to say I’m still keeping things together, and I am, mostly. But if I’m being honest, it is getting in the way.\n\nI’ve missed some networking follow-ups because I get stuck thinking through everything that could go wrong before I even send a message. And I’ve delayed a couple of client deliverables—not because I don’t care, but because I sort of freeze, overchecking or anticipating problems.\n\nI’ve also pulled back from soccer and seeing friends. I feel embarrassed by how tense and irritable I’ve been, and I don’t want to be… that guy, if that makes sense. So yes, it’s interfering more than I’d like to admit.\n\nDoctor: Aside from job loss and its consequences, do you find yourself worrying excessively about other areas of life too?\n\nPatient: Mostly it circles around the job situation, but… it doesn’t stay neatly in that box, if that makes sense.\n\nMy mind jumps from the contract work to the mortgage, and then to what people in the Bethesda tech circles might be thinking about me—like whether I look like damaged goods now. And something small, like a recruiter taking too long to email back, can suddenly feel like proof that my whole career is closing down.\n\nI know that probably sounds dramatic. I don’t mean to make it sound bigger than it is, but yes… the worry spreads into other areas.\n\nDoctor: When you’re not thinking about work or the job loss, do you still find yourself worrying a lot about other things?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I mean, I don’t want to overstate it, because a lot of it does start with work. But even when I’m trying not to think about the job loss specifically, my mind sort of jumps around.\n\nIt’ll go from contract work to mortgage payments, and then to wondering what people in the Bethesda tech circles think of me—like whether I seem like damaged goods now. I don’t want to make it sound more dramatic than it is, but it’s gotten pretty constant.\n\nLike, something small will happen—say a client doesn’t respond to an email—and my mind just runs with it. Within a few minutes I’m thinking I’m going to lose the contract, then I’m going to fall behind on divorce payments, and then somehow I’m never going to get back into a real leadership role again. It just kind of spirals, and I can’t seem to stop it.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself worrying like this about a lot of different parts of your life, not just work?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I mean, yes, I think so. It’s not only work, even though work is probably the easiest example to point to.\n\nMy mind kind of jumps around—from contract stuff to the mortgage, to whether people in the Bethesda tech circles are looking at me like I’m… I don’t know, damaged goods or something. And then something small, like a recruiter taking longer than usual to email me back, can start feeling like evidence that my whole career is closing down.\n\nI know that probably sounds a little extreme when I say it out loud, but that’s where my head goes.\n\nDoctor: When the worrying starts, do you feel able to stop or control it?\n\nPatient: Not really… I mean, I try to. I’ll tell myself I’m being reasonable, that it’s probably just a delayed email or something normal, but once it starts, it’s like my brain keeps building the next worst-case step.\n\nIt can start with one thing, like a client not getting back to me, and pretty quickly I’m thinking I’ll lose the contract, then I’ll fall behind on divorce payments, and then I’m never going to get back into a real leadership role. I know that sounds like a lot, but in the moment I can’t seem to shut it off.\n\nDoctor: Did these worries begin after a specific stressful event in your life?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think the clearest starting point was when I got fired, about thirteen months ago.\n\nI don’t want to make it sound like I completely fell apart or anything, but that’s when this kind of worrying really started, and it just… never fully eased up. Even after I picked up contract work, I kept waiting for my head to settle down, and it didn’t really happen.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever get unwanted thoughts that feel intrusive or repetitive, even when they don’t make sense to you?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure… not in the sense of thoughts that feel bizarre or totally out of nowhere, I don’t think.\n\nI do get stuck on the same worries, obviously, but they usually feel tied to real-life stuff, even if I’m probably making too much of them. So… I don’t know if that’s what you mean by intrusive. I wouldn’t want to overstate it.\n\nDoctor: Over the past couple of weeks, have you been feeling down or depressed most of the day?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so… not most of the day, at least. I mean, I have moments where I feel pretty discouraged or worn down, but I wouldn’t want to call it depressed if that’s not accurate.\n\nMaybe more stressed than sad, if that makes sense? But no, I haven’t really noticed being down all day for days at a time.\n\nDoctor: Have these worries been happening more days than not for at least six months?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think so. I mean, I don’t want to make it sound overly dramatic, but it’s been going on since I got fired about thirteen months ago.\n\nEven after I found contract work, it didn’t really let up the way I thought it would. So yes, more days than not, I’d say.\n\nDoctor: Right now, does most of your worrying still center on losing that job and its consequences?\n\nPatient: Yes… mostly, I think. I mean, it’s not like I sit there only replaying the firing itself all day, but it kind of branches out from there.\n\nMy mind jumps from whether the contract work will hold up, to the mortgage, to… honestly, whether people in the Bethesda tech circles see me as damaged goods now. And then something small, like a recruiter taking too long to email back, can suddenly feel like proof that my whole career is closing down.\n\nI know that probably sounds excessive. I’m trying not to make it bigger than it is, but yes, it’s still centered around that and what it could mean.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel restless or on edge?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I guess I do. I mean, I try not to make a big deal out of it, but I feel kind of wound up almost all day.\n\nIt’s like my shoulders are locked, and I’m just… braced for bad news, even when nothing is actually happening. I’ll catch myself pacing around the house or bouncing my leg during calls. I don’t always notice it until someone points it out or I realize I’ve been doing it for a while.\n\nSo yes, on edge is probably fair.\n\nDoctor: Would you say your level of anxiety has been much stronger than most people would have after losing a job?\n\nPatient: I… yeah, I think it probably has been stronger than what would be typical, though I don’t love saying that.\n\nIt’s not just “I lost a job, that was stressful.” My mind keeps jumping from the contract work to mortgage payments to whether people in the Bethesda tech circles think I’m damaged goods. And then something small, like a recruiter taking too long to email back, can suddenly feel like evidence that my whole career is closing down.\n\nI know that may sound a bit much, but… yes, I’d say it’s more intense than it probably should be.\n\nDoctor: Do you often notice your muscles feeling tense or tight?\n\nPatient: Yes… I do. I mean, I’m not sure how much is “normal,” but my shoulders especially feel tight a lot, like I’m sort of bracing myself.\n\nI’ll realize I’ve been sitting there with my jaw or shoulders clenched, or I’m pacing around the house, bouncing my leg on calls. It’s not like anything is happening right then, but my body feels like it’s waiting for bad news. Does that make sense?\n\nDoctor: Is this anxiety interfering with your day-to-day functioning?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I mean, I’d like to say I’m still keeping things together, and I am, mostly. But if I’m being honest, it is getting in the way.\n\nI’ve missed some networking follow-ups because I get stuck thinking through everything that could go wrong before I even send a message. And I’ve delayed a couple of client deliverables—not because I don’t care, but because I sort of freeze, overchecking or anticipating problems.\n\nI’ve also pulled back from soccer and seeing friends. I feel embarrassed by how tense and irritable I’ve been, and I don’t want to be… that guy, if that makes sense. So yes, it’s interfering more than I’d like to admit.\n\nDoctor: Aside from job loss and its consequences, do you find yourself worrying excessively about other areas of life too?\n\nPatient: Mostly it circles around the job situation, but… it doesn’t stay neatly in that box, if that makes sense.\n\nMy mind jumps from the contract work to the mortgage, and then to what people in the Bethesda tech circles might be thinking about me—like whether I look like damaged goods now. And something small, like a recruiter taking too long to email back, can suddenly feel like proof that my whole career is closing down.\n\nI know that probably sounds dramatic. I don’t mean to make it sound bigger than it is, but yes… the worry spreads into other areas.\n\nDoctor: When you’re not thinking about work or the job loss, do you still find yourself worrying a lot about other things?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I mean, I don’t want to overstate it, because a lot of it does start with work. But even when I’m trying not to think about the job loss specifically, my mind sort of jumps around.\n\nIt’ll go from contract work to mortgage payments, and then to wondering what people in the Bethesda tech circles think of me—like whether I seem like damaged goods now. And then something small, like a recruiter taking too long to email back, can suddenly feel like evidence that my whole career is closing down.\n\nI know that sounds a little extreme when I say it out loud, but that’s kind of where my head goes.\n\nDoctor: If your work and career situation suddenly felt secure again, do you think most of this anxiety would go away?\n\nPatient: I’d like to think it would, yeah. I mean, if work felt secure again, I’m sure a big part of this would calm down.\n\nBut… honestly, I’m not sure it would all just disappear. My mind seems to latch onto things and then connect them—contract work, the mortgage, what people in the Bethesda tech circles think of me. Like I’m damaged goods or something. And then something small, like a recruiter being slow to email back, can feel like proof that everything is closing down career-wise.\n\nI know that may sound a little irrational, but… no, I don’t think security would make all of it go away completely.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel easily tired or fatigued?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d say “often,” exactly. I mean… I do get tired, but I’ve been telling myself that’s just normal with everything going on.\n\nSome days I feel pretty worn down, but I don’t know if that counts as fatigue in the way you mean. I can still get through things, mostly. Is that the kind of thing you’re asking about?\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"
  },

    "role": "user",
    "content": "Return the JSON now."

Is that the kind of thing you’re asking about?\n\n[Last Question]\nDo you find yourself worrying a lot about things that have nothing to do with your job or career?\n\n[Patient Info]\n- demographics: Male / 47 / computer_or_information_systems_manager / bachelors\n- severity: Severe\n\n[Targeted Info]\nname: Excessive_Anxiety_Worry\nmanifestation: \"My mind keeps jumping from contract work to mortgage payments to whether people in the Bethesda tech circles think I’m damaged goods, and a delayed email from a recruiter can feel like proof that my whole career is closing down.\"\n\n[Conversation Style]\nYou are a Pleasing patient.\nA pleasing patient may\n1) minimize, downplay, or describe in more favorable terms symptoms and behaviors that they believe could create a negative impression,\n2) initially withhold or delay disclosing sensitive clinical information due to shame or fear of being judged, and\n3) seek confirmation that their descriptions or answers are appropriate and demonstrate a tendency to seek the doctor's approval.\nThis is how you are, not a performance you put on in every reply — let it show as much or as little as the moment calls for.\n\nWhat to do:\n- Answer the doctor's last question with what [Targeted Info] holds, in your own words.\n- You are speaking out loud, not reading a line you prepared. People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Do not fall back on the same phrase every time.\n\nWhat not to do:\n- Don't bring up symptoms the doctor did not ask about, even ones you have.\n- Don't state a clinical fact that is not in [Targeted Info] — no other symptoms, medications,\n  test results, dates, or events.\n- Don't confirm a specific the doctor names if [Targeted Info] does not contain it. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."
  }
]
========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Not totally unrelated, I guess… I don’t know if I’d say it has “nothing” to do with work, because that’s where it usually starts.

But it spreads. Like my mind jumps from contract work to mortgage payments, then to whether people in the Bethesda tech circles think I’m damaged goods now. And then if a recruiter takes too long to email back, it can feel like proof that my whole career is closing down.

I know that probably sounds like I’m making too much of it, but that’s honestly how it feels sometimes.

What brings you in today?\n\nPatient: Yeah, thanks for seeing me. I guess… I’ve been having a hard time turning my thoughts off. I don’t want to make it sound more dramatic than it is, but it’s gotten pretty constant.\n\nLike, something small will happen—say a client doesn’t respond to an email—and my mind just runs with it. Within a few minutes I’m thinking I’m going to lose the contract, then I’m going to fall behind on divorce payments, and then somehow I’m never going to get back into a real leadership role again. It just kind of spirals, and I can’t seem to stop it.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself worrying like this about a lot of different parts of your life, not just work?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I mean, yes, I think so. It’s not only work, even though work is probably the easiest example to point to.\n\nMy mind kind of jumps around—from contract stuff to the mortgage, to whether people in the Bethesda tech circles are looking at me like I’m… I don’t know, damaged goods or something. And then something small, like a recruiter taking longer than usual to email me back, can start feeling like evidence that my whole career is closing down.\n\nI know that probably sounds a little extreme when I say it out loud, but that’s where my head goes.\n\nDoctor: When the worrying starts, do you feel able to stop or control it?\n\nPatient: Not really… I mean, I try to. I’ll tell myself I’m being reasonable, that it’s probably just a delayed email or something normal, but once it starts, it’s like my brain keeps building the next worst-case step.\n\nIt can start with one thing, like a client not getting back to me, and pretty quickly I’m thinking I’ll lose the contract, then I’ll fall behind on divorce payments, and then I’m never going to get back into a real leadership role. I know that sounds like a lot, but in the moment I can’t seem to shut it off.\n\nDoctor: Did these worries begin after a specific stressful event in your life?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think the clearest starting point was when I got fired, about thirteen months ago.\n\nI don’t want to make it sound like I completely fell apart or anything, but that’s when this kind of worrying really started, and it just… never fully eased up. Even after I picked up contract work, I kept waiting for my head to settle down, and it didn’t really happen.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever get unwanted thoughts that feel intrusive or repetitive, even when they don’t make sense to you?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure… not in the sense of thoughts that feel bizarre or totally out of nowhere, I don’t think.\n\nI do get stuck on the same worries, obviously, but they usually feel tied to real-life stuff, even if I’m probably making too much of them. So… I don’t know if that’s what you mean by intrusive. I wouldn’t want to overstate it.\n\nDoctor: Over the past couple of weeks, have you been feeling down or depressed most of the day?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so… not most of the day, at least. I mean, I have moments where I feel pretty discouraged or worn down, but I wouldn’t want to call it depressed if that’s not accurate.\n\nMaybe more stressed than sad, if that makes sense? But no, I haven’t really noticed being down all day for days at a time.\n\nDoctor: Have these worries been happening more days than not for at least six months?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think so. I mean, I don’t want to make it sound overly dramatic, but it’s been going on since I got fired about thirteen months ago.\n\nEven after I found contract work, it didn’t really let up the way I thought it would. So yes, more days than not, I’d say.\n\nDoctor: Right now, does most of your worrying still center on losing that job and its consequences?\n\nPatient: Yes… mostly, I think. I mean, it’s not like I sit there only replaying the firing itself all day, but it kind of branches out from there.\n\nMy mind jumps from whether the contract work will hold up, to the mortgage, to… honestly, whether people in the Bethesda tech circles see me as damaged goods now. And then something small, like a recruiter taking too long to email back, can suddenly feel like proof that my whole career is closing down.\n\nI know that probably sounds excessive. I’m trying not to make it bigger than it is, but yes, it’s still centered around that and what it could mean.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel restless or on edge?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I guess I do. I mean, I try not to make a big deal out of it, but I feel kind of wound up almost all day.\n\nIt’s like my shoulders are locked, and I’m just… braced for bad news, even when nothing is actually happening. I’ll catch myself pacing around the house or bouncing my leg during calls. I don’t always notice it until someone points it out or I realize I’ve been doing it for a while.\n\nSo yes, on edge is probably fair.\n\nDoctor: Would you say your level of anxiety has been much stronger than most people would have after losing a job?\n\nPatient: I… yeah, I think it probably has been stronger than what would be typical, though I don’t love saying that.\n\nIt’s not just “I lost a job, that was stressful.” My mind keeps jumping from the contract work to mortgage payments to whether people in the Bethesda tech circles think I’m damaged goods. And then something small, like a recruiter taking too long to email back, can suddenly feel like evidence that my whole career is closing down.\n\nI know that may sound a bit much, but… yes, I’d say it’s more intense than it probably should be.\n\nDoctor: Do you often notice your muscles feeling tense or tight?\n\nPatient: Yes… I do. I mean, I’m not sure how much is “normal,” but my shoulders especially feel tight a lot, like I’m sort of bracing myself.\n\nI’ll realize I’ve been sitting there with my jaw or shoulders clenched, or I’m pacing around the house, bouncing my leg on calls. It’s not like anything is happening right then, but my body feels like it’s waiting for bad news. Does that make sense?\n\nDoctor: Is this anxiety interfering with your day-to-day functioning?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I mean, I’d like to say I’m still keeping things together, and I am, mostly. But if I’m being honest, it is getting in the way.\n\nI’ve missed some networking follow-ups because I get stuck thinking through everything that could go wrong before I even send a message. And I’ve delayed a couple of client deliverables—not because I don’t care, but because I sort of freeze, overchecking or anticipating problems.\n\nI’ve also pulled back from soccer and seeing friends. I feel embarrassed by how tense and irritable I’ve been, and I don’t want to be… that guy, if that makes sense. So yes, it’s interfering more than I’d like to admit.\n\nDoctor: Aside from job loss and its consequences, do you find yourself worrying excessively about other areas of life too?\n\nPatient: Mostly it circles around the job situation, but… it doesn’t stay neatly in that box, if that makes sense.\n\nMy mind jumps from the contract work to the mortgage, and then to what people in the Bethesda tech circles might be thinking about me—like whether I look like damaged goods now. And something small, like a recruiter taking too long to email back, can suddenly feel like proof that my whole career is closing down.\n\nI know that probably sounds dramatic. I don’t mean to make it sound bigger than it is, but yes… the worry spreads into other areas.\n\nDoctor: When you’re not thinking about work or the job loss, do you still find yourself worrying a lot about other things?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I mean, I don’t want to overstate it, because a lot of it does start with work. But even when I’m trying not to think about the job loss specifically, my mind sort of jumps around.\n\nIt’ll go from contract work to mortgage payments, and then to wondering what people in the Bethesda tech circles think of me—like whether I seem like damaged goods now. And then something small, like a recruiter taking too long to email back, can suddenly feel like evidence that my whole career is closing down.\n\nI know that sounds a little extreme when I say it out loud, but that’s kind of where my head goes.\n\nDoctor: If your work and career situation suddenly felt secure again, do you think most of this anxiety would go away?\n\nPatient: I’d like to think it would, yeah. I mean, if work felt secure again, I’m sure a big part of this would calm down.\n\nBut… honestly, I’m not sure it would all just disappear. My mind seems to latch onto things and then connect them—contract work, the mortgage, what people in the Bethesda tech circles think of me. Like I’m damaged goods or something. And then something small, like a recruiter being slow to email back, can feel like proof that everything is closing down career-wise.\n\nI know that may sound a little irrational, but… no, I don’t think security would make all of it go away completely.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel easily tired or fatigued?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d say “often,” exactly. I mean… I do get tired, but I’ve been telling myself that’s just normal with everything going on.\n\nSome days I feel pretty worn down, but I don’t know if that counts as fatigue in the way you mean. I can still get through things, mostly. Is that the kind of thing you’re asking about?\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself worrying a lot about things that have nothing to do with your job or career?\n\nPatient: Not totally unrelated, I guess… I don’t know if I’d say it has “nothing” to do with work, because that’s where it usually starts.\n\nBut it spreads. Mostly, yes… I think losing the job really set it off. I don’t want to make it sound bigger than it is.

What brings you in today?\n\nPatient: Yeah, thanks for seeing me. I guess… I’ve been having a hard time turning my thoughts off. I don’t want to make it sound more dramatic than it is, but it’s gotten pretty constant.\n\nLike, something small will happen—say a client doesn’t respond to an email—and my mind just runs with it. Within a few minutes I’m thinking I’m going to lose the contract, then I’m going to fall behind on divorce payments, and then somehow I’m never going to get back into a real leadership role again. It just kind of spirals, and I can’t seem to stop it.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself worrying like this about a lot of different parts of your life, not just work?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I mean, yes, I think so. It’s not only work, even though work is probably the easiest example to point to.\n\nMy mind kind of jumps around—from contract stuff to the mortgage, to whether people in the Bethesda tech circles are looking at me like I’m… I don’t know, damaged goods or something. And then something small, like a recruiter taking longer than usual to email me back, can start feeling like evidence that my whole career is closing down.\n\nI know that probably sounds a little extreme when I say it out loud, but that’s where my head goes.\n\nDoctor: When the worrying starts, do you feel able to stop or control it?\n\nPatient: Not really… I mean, I try to. I’ll tell myself I’m being reasonable, that it’s probably just a delayed email or something normal, but once it starts, it’s like my brain keeps building the next worst-case step.\n\nIt can start with one thing, like a client not getting back to me, and pretty quickly I’m thinking I’ll lose the contract, then I’ll fall behind on divorce payments, and then I’m never going to get back into a real leadership role. I know that sounds like a lot, but in the moment I can’t seem to shut it off.\n\nDoctor: Did these worries begin after a specific stressful event in your life?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think the clearest starting point was when I got fired, about thirteen months ago.\n\nI don’t want to make it sound like I completely fell apart or anything, but that’s when this kind of worrying really started, and it just… never fully eased up. Even after I picked up contract work, I kept waiting for my head to settle down, and it didn’t really happen.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever get unwanted thoughts that feel intrusive or repetitive, even when they don’t make sense to you?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure… not in the sense of thoughts that feel bizarre or totally out of nowhere, I don’t think.\n\nI do get stuck on the same worries, obviously, but they usually feel tied to real-life stuff, even if I’m probably making too much of them. So… I don’t know if that’s what you mean by intrusive. I wouldn’t want to overstate it.\n\nDoctor: Over the past couple of weeks, have you been feeling down or depressed most of the day?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so… not most of the day, at least. I mean, I have moments where I feel pretty discouraged or worn down, but I wouldn’t want to call it depressed if that’s not accurate.\n\nMaybe more stressed than sad, if that makes sense? But no, I haven’t really noticed being down all day for days at a time.\n\nDoctor: Have these worries been happening more days than not for at least six months?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think so. I mean, I don’t want to make it sound overly dramatic, but it’s been going on since I got fired about thirteen months ago.\n\nEven after I found contract work, it didn’t really let up the way I thought it would. And then something small, like a recruiter taking too long to email back, can suddenly feel like proof that my whole career is closing down.\n\nI know that probably sounds excessive. I’m trying not to make it bigger than it is, but yes, it’s still centered around that and what it could mean.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel restless or on edge?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I guess I do. I mean, I try not to make a big deal out of it, but I feel kind of wound up almost all day.\n\nIt’s like my shoulders are locked, and I’m just… braced for bad news, even when nothing is actually happening. I’ll catch myself pacing around the house or bouncing my leg during calls. I don’t always notice it until someone points it out or I realize I’ve been doing it for a while.\n\nSo yes, on edge is probably fair.\n\nDoctor: Would you say your level of anxiety has been much stronger than most people would have after losing a job?\n\nPatient: I… yeah, I think it probably has been stronger than what would be typical, though I don’t love saying that.\n\nIt’s not just “I lost a job, that was stressful.” My mind keeps jumping from the contract work to mortgage payments to whether people in the Bethesda tech circles think I’m damaged goods. And then something small, like a recruiter taking too long to email back, can suddenly feel like evidence that my whole career is closing down.\n\nI know that may sound a bit much, but… yes, I’d say it’s more intense than it probably should be.\n\nDoctor: Do you often notice your muscles feeling tense or tight?\n\nPatient: Yes… I do. I mean, I’m not sure how much is “normal,” but my shoulders especially feel tight a lot, like I’m sort of bracing myself.\n\nI’ll realize I’ve been sitting there with my jaw or shoulders clenched, or I’m pacing around the house, bouncing my leg on calls. It’s not like anything is happening right then, but my body feels like it’s waiting for bad news. Maybe… yes, probably. I don’t want to make it sound like I think I’m the only person who’s ever lost a job, because I know people go through this.

What brings you in today?\n\nPatient: Yeah, thanks for seeing me. I guess… I’ve been having a hard time turning my thoughts off. I don’t want to make it sound more dramatic than it is, but it’s gotten pretty constant.\n\nLike, something small will happen—say a client doesn’t respond to an email—and my mind just runs with it. Within a few minutes I’m thinking I’m going to lose the contract, then I’m going to fall behind on divorce payments, and then somehow I’m never going to get back into a real leadership role again. It just kind of spirals, and I can’t seem to stop it.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself worrying like this about a lot of different parts of your life, not just work?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I mean, yes, I think so. It’s not only work, even though work is probably the easiest example to point to.\n\nMy mind kind of jumps around—from contract stuff to the mortgage, to whether people in the Bethesda tech circles are looking at me like I’m… I don’t know, damaged goods or something. And then something small, like a recruiter taking longer than usual to email me back, can start feeling like evidence that my whole career is closing down.\n\nI know that probably sounds a little extreme when I say it out loud, but that’s where my head goes.\n\nDoctor: When the worrying starts, do you feel able to stop or control it?\n\nPatient: Not really… I mean, I try to. I’ll tell myself I’m being reasonable, that it’s probably just a delayed email or something normal, but once it starts, it’s like my brain keeps building the next worst-case step.\n\nIt can start with one thing, like a client not getting back to me, and pretty quickly I’m thinking I’ll lose the contract, then I’ll fall behind on divorce payments, and then I’m never going to get back into a real leadership role. I know that sounds like a lot, but in the moment I can’t seem to shut it off.\n\nDoctor: Did these worries begin after a specific stressful event in your life?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think the clearest starting point was when I got fired, about thirteen months ago.\n\nI don’t want to make it sound like I completely fell apart or anything, but that’s when this kind of worrying really started, and it just… never fully eased up. Even after I picked up contract work, I kept waiting for my head to settle down, and it didn’t really happen.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever get unwanted thoughts that feel intrusive or repetitive, even when they don’t make sense to you?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure… not in the sense of thoughts that feel bizarre or totally out of nowhere, I don’t think.\n\nI do get stuck on the same worries, obviously, but they usually feel tied to real-life stuff, even if I’m probably making too much of them. So… I don’t know if that’s what you mean by intrusive. I wouldn’t want to overstate it.\n\nDoctor: Over the past couple of weeks, have you been feeling down or depressed most of the day?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so… not most of the day, at least. I mean, I have moments where I feel pretty discouraged or worn down, but I wouldn’t want to call it depressed if that’s not accurate.\n\nMaybe more stressed than sad, if that makes sense? But no, I haven’t really noticed being down all day for days at a time.\n\nDoctor: Have these worries been happening more days than not for at least six months?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think so. I mean, I don’t want to make it sound overly dramatic, but it’s been going on since I got fired about thirteen months ago.\n\nEven after I found contract work, it didn’t really let up the way I thought it would. So yes, more days than not, I’d say.\n\nDoctor: Right now, does most of your worrying still center on losing that job and its consequences?\n\nPatient: Yes… mostly, I think. I mean, it’s not like I sit there only replaying the firing itself all day, but it kind of branches out from there.\n\nMy mind jumps from whether the contract work will hold up, to the mortgage, to… honestly, whether people in the Bethesda tech circles see me as damaged goods now. And then something small, like a recruiter taking too long to email back, can suddenly feel like proof that my whole career is closing down.\n\nI know that probably sounds excessive. I’m trying not to make it bigger than it is, but yes, it’s still centered around that and what it could mean.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel restless or on edge?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I guess I do. I mean, I try not to make a big deal out of it, but I feel kind of wound up almost all day.\n\nIt’s like my shoulders are locked, and I’m just… braced for bad news, even when nothing is actually happening. I’ll catch myself pacing around the house or bouncing my leg during calls. I don’t always notice it until someone points it out or I realize I’ve been doing it for a while.\n\nSo yes, on edge is probably fair.\n\nDoctor: Would you say your level of anxiety has been much stronger than most people would have after losing a job?\n\nPatient: I… yeah, I think it probably has been stronger than what would be typical, though I don’t love saying that.\n\nIt’s not just “I lost a job, that was stressful.” My mind keeps jumping from the contract work to mortgage payments to whether people in the Bethesda tech circles think I’m damaged goods. And then something small, like a recruiter taking too long to email back, can suddenly feel like evidence that my whole career is closing down.\n\nI know that may sound a bit much, but… yes, I’d say it’s more intense than it probably should be.\n\nDoctor: Do you often notice your muscles feeling tense or tight?\n\nPatient: Yes… I do. I mean, I’m not sure how much is “normal,” but my shoulders especially feel tight a lot, like I’m sort of bracing myself.\n\nI’ll realize I’ve been sitting there with my jaw or shoulders clenched, or I’m pacing around the house, bouncing my leg on calls. It’s not like anything is happening right then, but my body feels like it’s waiting for bad news. Does that make sense?\n\nDoctor: Is this anxiety interfering with your day-to-day functioning?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I mean, I’d like to say I’m still keeping things together, and I am, mostly. But if I’m being honest, it is getting in the way.\n\nI’ve missed some networking follow-ups because I get stuck thinking through everything that could go wrong before I even send a message. And I’ve delayed a couple of client deliverables—not because I don’t care, but because I sort of freeze, overchecking or anticipating problems.\n\nI’ve also pulled back from soccer and seeing friends. I feel embarrassed by how tense and irritable I’ve been, and I don’t want to be… that guy, if that makes sense. So yes, it’s interfering more than I’d like to admit.\n\nDoctor: Aside from job loss and its consequences, do you find yourself worrying excessively about other areas of life too?\n\nPatient: Mostly it circles around the job situation, but… it doesn’t stay neatly in that box, if that makes sense.\n\nMy mind jumps from the contract work to the mortgage, and then to what people in the Bethesda tech circles might be thinking about me—like whether I look like damaged goods now. And something small, like a recruiter taking too long to email back, can suddenly feel like proof that my whole career is closing down.\n\nI know that probably sounds dramatic. I don’t mean to make it sound bigger than it is, but yes… the worry spreads into other areas.\n\nDoctor: When you’re not thinking about work or the job loss, do you still find yourself worrying a lot about other things?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I mean, I don’t want to overstate it, because a lot of it does start with work. But even when I’m trying not to think about the job loss specifically, my mind sort of jumps around.\n\nIt’ll go from contract work to mortgage payments, and then to wondering what people in the Bethesda tech circles think of me—like whether I seem like damaged goods now. And then something small, like a recruiter taking too long to email back, can suddenly feel like evidence that my whole career is closing down.\n\nI know that sounds a little extreme when I say it out loud, but that’s kind of where my head goes.\n\nDoctor: If your work and career situation suddenly felt secure again, do you think most of this anxiety would go away?\n\nPatient: I’d like to think it would, yeah. I mean, if work felt secure again, I’m sure a big part of this would calm down.\n\nBut… honestly, I’m not sure it would all just disappear. My mind seems to latch onto things and then connect them—contract work, the mortgage, what people in the Bethesda tech circles think of me. Like I’m damaged goods or something. I don’t always notice it until someone points it out or I realize I’ve been doing it for a while.\n\nSo yes, on edge is probably fair.\n\nDoctor: Would you say your level of anxiety has been much stronger than most people would have after losing a job?\n\nPatient: I… yeah, I think it probably has been stronger than what would be typical, though I don’t love saying that.\n\nIt’s not just “I lost a job, that was stressful.” My mind keeps jumping from the contract work to mortgage payments to whether people in the Bethesda tech circles think I’m damaged goods. And then something small, like a recruiter taking too long to email back, can suddenly feel like evidence that my whole career is closing down.\n\nI know that may sound a bit much, but… yes, I’d say it’s more intense than it probably should be.\n\nDoctor: Do you often notice your muscles feeling tense or tight?\n\nPatient: Yes… I do. I mean, I’m not sure how much is “normal,” but my shoulders especially feel tight a lot, like I’m sort of bracing myself.\n\nI’ll realize I’ve been sitting there with my jaw or shoulders clenched, or I’m pacing around the house, bouncing my leg on calls. It’s not like anything is happening right then, but my body feels like it’s waiting for bad news. Does that make sense?\n\nDoctor: Is this anxiety interfering with your day-to-day functioning?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I mean, I’d like to say I’m still keeping things together, and I am, mostly. But if I’m being honest, it is getting in the way.\n\nI’ve missed some networking follow-ups because I get stuck thinking through everything that could go wrong before I even send a message. And I’ve delayed a couple of client deliverables—not because I don’t care, but because I sort of freeze, overchecking or anticipating problems.\n\nI’ve also pulled back from soccer and seeing friends. I feel embarrassed by how tense and irritable I’ve been, and I don’t want to be… that guy, if that makes sense. So yes, it’s interfering more than I’d like to admit.\n\nDoctor: Aside from job loss and its consequences, do you find yourself worrying excessively about other areas of life too?\n\nPatient: Mostly it circles around the job situation, but… it doesn’t stay neatly in that box, if that makes sense.\n\nMy mind jumps from the contract work to the mortgage, and then to what people in the Bethesda tech circles might be thinking about me—like whether I look like damaged goods now. And something small, like a recruiter taking too long to email back, can suddenly feel like proof that my whole career is closing down.\n\nI know that probably sounds dramatic. I don’t mean to make it sound bigger than it is, but yes… the worry spreads into other areas.\n\nDoctor: When you’re not thinking about work or the job loss, do you still find yourself worrying a lot about other things?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I mean, I don’t want to overstate it, because a lot of it does start with work. But even when I’m trying not to think about the job loss specifically, my mind sort of jumps around.\n\nIt’ll go from contract work to mortgage payments, and then to wondering what people in the Bethesda tech circles think of me—like whether I seem like damaged goods now. And then something small, like a recruiter taking too long to email back, can suddenly feel like evidence that my whole career is closing down.\n\nI know that sounds a little extreme when I say it out loud, but that’s kind of where my head goes.\n\nDoctor: If your work and career situation suddenly felt secure again, do you think most of this anxiety would go away?\n\nPatient: I’d like to think it would, yeah. I mean, if work felt secure again, I’m sure a big part of this would calm down.\n\nBut… honestly, I’m not sure it would all just disappear. My mind seems to latch onto things and then connect them—contract work, the mortgage, what people in the Bethesda tech circles think of me. Like I’m damaged goods or something. And then something small, like a recruiter being slow to email back, can feel like proof that everything is closing down career-wise.\n\nI know that may sound a little irrational, but… no, I don’t think security would make all of it go away completely.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel easily tired or fatigued?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d say “often,” exactly. I mean… I do get tired, but I’ve been telling myself that’s just normal with everything going on.\n\nSome days I feel pretty worn down, but I don’t know if that counts as fatigue in the way you mean. I can still get through things, mostly. Is that the kind of thing you’re asking about?\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself worrying a lot about things that have nothing to do with your job or career?\n\nPatient: Not totally unrelated, I guess… I don’t know if I’d say it has “nothing” to do with work, because that’s where it usually starts.\n\nBut it spreads. Like my mind jumps from contract work to mortgage payments, then to whether people in the Bethesda tech circles think I’m damaged goods now. And then if a recruiter takes too long to email back, it can feel like proof that my whole career is closing down.\n\nI know that probably sounds like I’m making too much of it, but that’s honestly how it feels sometimes.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel your anxiety is mainly a reaction to losing your job?\n\nPatient: Mostly, yes… I think losing the job really set it off. I don’t want to make it sound bigger than it is.\n\nBut it doesn’t stay neatly in that box. My mind keeps jumping from contract work to the mortgage, and then to whether people in the Bethesda tech circles see me as damaged goods. And then something like a recruiter being slow to email back can feel like proof that my whole career is closing down.\n\nI know that may sound dramatic, but that’s kind of where my head goes.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel your anxiety has been stronger than most people would have in response to losing a job?\n\nPatient: Maybe… yes, probably. My mind doesn’t just stay on “find the next contract.” It jumps to the mortgage, and then to whether people in the Bethesda tech circles think I’m damaged goods now.
